In order to see that Theorem
6.16
(
2
) implies this result we first note that in this case

d=2^{t}+e,\quad 1\leq e\leq 2^{t}-2,\quad 2^{a_{1}}z=2^{\gamma(d)}-d-1=2^{t}-e-1.

Now, as in the previous situations we consider the following difference and show that it is non-negative:

\big((d-2e-1)(\ell-\alpha(\ell))+(d+1)\ell-2\big)-\\
\Big(\frac{1}{2}\big(2^{\gamma(d)}-d-1+2^{a_{1}}\big)(\ell-\epsilon(\ell))-2^{a_{1}}\alpha(\ell)+(d+1)\ell-2\Big)=

(2^{t}-e-1)(\ell-\alpha(\ell))\\
-\frac{1}{2}\big(2^{t}-e-1\big)(\ell-\epsilon(\ell))-2^{a_{1}-1}(\ell-\epsilon(\ell))+2^{a_{1}}\alpha(\ell)=

2^{a_{1}-1}z(\ell-2\alpha(\ell)+\epsilon(\ell))-2^{a_{1}-1}(\ell-2\alpha(\ell)-\epsilon(\ell))=\\
2^{a_{1}-1}\big((z-1)(\ell-2\alpha(\ell))+(z+1)\epsilon(\ell)\big)\geq 0.

This concludes the proof of the corollary.
∎

In the same way as previous two corollaries we can show the following.

Corollary 6.19
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
k\geq 1
and
\ell\geq 1
be integers, and let
d=2^{t}+e
for some integers
t\geq 1
and
0\leq e\leq 2^{t}-1
. Then

Theorem
6.16
(
3
)

\Longrightarrow
Theorem
5.22
.

6.4.
Additional bounds for the existence of complex highly regular embeddings

In this section we derive additional consequence of Lemma
6.6
which yields further bounds for the existence of complex highly regular embeddings.
In particular, we will improve bounds given in Theorem
5.26
and Theorem
5.28
.

Let us denote by
\lambda_{d,m}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\lambda_{d,m}
and
\psi^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}]:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\psi_{d,m}
complex versions of the real vector bundles
\lambda_{d,m}
and
\psi_{d,m}[r_{1},\dots,r_{m}]
.
Note that there is an isomorphism of real vector bundles
\lambda_{d,m}^{\mathbb{C}}\cong\lambda_{d,m}^{\oplus 2}
and

\psi^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}]\cong\psi_{d,m}[2r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,2r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}].

The following lemma is a consequence of Lemma
6.6
.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
m\geq 1
,
d^{\mathbb{C}}\geq 1
and
{\ell}^{\mathbb{C}}\geq 1
be integers, and let
r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}
be non-negative integers.
If the binomial coefficient

{(r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{m-2}+\dots+r_{m-1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{0})+r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}-(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1+\ell^{\mathbb{C}})(2^{m-1}-1)-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\choose{d^{\mathbb{C}}-1}

(99)

is odd, then

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}((\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}})^{-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\psi_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}}[r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}])\neq 0,

as an element of the cohomology group
H^{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}(\operatorname{Pe}(\mathbb{R}^{d},2^{m})/\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}};\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
Here
(\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}})^{-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}
denotes an inverse of the vector bundle
(\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}})^{\oplus\ell}
.

Proof.

There is isomorphism of real vector bundles

(\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}})^{-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\psi_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}}[r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}]\cong\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{-2\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\psi_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}[2r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,2r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}].

Thus, it suffices to prove that

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}(\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{-2\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\psi_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}[2r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,2r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}])\neq 0.

If we set that
d=2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1
,
\ell=2\ell^{\mathbb{C}}
, and
r_{1}:=2r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{1}:=2r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}
, then by Lemma
6.6
we have that the Stiefel--Whitney class

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}(\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{-2\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\psi_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}[2r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,2r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}])=\\
w_{(d-1)(2^{m}-1)}(\lambda_{d,m}^{-\ell}\oplus\psi_{d,m}[r_{1},\dots,r_{m}])\neq 0

does not vanish if and only if the binomial coefficient

{{(r_{1}2^{m-2}+\dots+r_{m-1}2^{0})+r_{m}-(d-1+\ell)(2^{m-1}-1)-\ell}\choose{d-1}}=\\
{{2\big((r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{m-2}+\dots+r_{m-1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{0})+r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}-(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1+\ell^{\mathbb{C}})(2^{m-1}-1)-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}\big)}\choose{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)}}

is odd.
Since the binomial coefficient
\binom{2a}{2b}
is odd if and only if the binomial coefficient
\binom{a}{b}
is odd, then the assumption (
99
) implies that the binomial coefficient

{{2\big((r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{m-2}+\dots+r_{m-1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{0})+r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}-(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1+\ell^{\mathbb{C}})(2^{m-1}-1)-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}\big)}\choose{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)}}

is odd.
Hence,

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}((\lambda_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}})^{-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}}\oplus\psi_{2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1,m}^{\mathbb{C}}[r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}},\dots,r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}])\neq 0.

∎

Let us denote by
\mu_{d,m}^{\mathbb{C}}[j]:=\mathbb{C}\otimes\mu_{d,m}[j]
, for all
1\leq j\leq m
, the complex version of the real vector bundle
\mu_{d,m}[j]
.
There is an isomorphism of real vector bundles
\mu_{d,m}^{\mathbb{C}}[j]\cong\mu_{d,m}[j]^{\oplus 2}
.
Now, like in the case of Lemma
6.9
and Corollary
6.10
, we deduce the following consequence of Lemma
6.20
.

Corollary 6.21
.

Let
d^{\mathbb{C}}\geq 2
and
m\geq 1
be integers, and let
d^{\mathbb{C}}=2^{t}+e
for some integer
t\geq 1
and
0\leq e\leq 2^{t}-1
.
If
d=2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1
, then

(1)

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}\big((\lambda^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m})^{-1}\oplus(\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[2]\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[m])^{\oplus 2e}\big)\not=0
,

(2)

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}((\lambda^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m})^{-(d^{\mathbb{C}}+1)}\oplus(\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[1]\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[m])^{\oplus 2e})\not=0
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of Lemma
6.9
.
For the sake of completeness we present the proof in detail.

(1)
Let
\ell^{\mathbb{C}}=1
,
r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}=0
, and
r_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}=\dots=r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}=2e
.
Then

(r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{m-2}+\dots+r_{m-1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{0})+r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}-(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1+\ell^{\mathbb{C}})(2^{m-1}-1)-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}=\\
2e(2^{m-3}+\dots+2^{0})+2e-d^{\mathbb{C}}(2^{m-1}-1)-1=2^{m-1}(e-d^{\mathbb{C}})+2e+d^{\mathbb{C}}-1=\\
2^{m-1}(e-d^{\mathbb{C}})+d^{\mathbb{C}}-1=\\
\begin{cases}d^{\mathbb{C}}-1-2^{m-1+t},&m\geq 2,\\
-1,&m=1.\end{cases}

Since
{{-1}\choose{d^{\mathbb{C}}-1}}=_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}1\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
{d^{\mathbb{C}}-1-2^{m-1+t}\choose d^{\mathbb{C}}-1}=_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}{d^{\mathbb{C}}-1\choose d^{\mathbb{C}}-1}=_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}1\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the assumption of Lemma
6.20
is satisfied for the chosen parameters.
Consequently,

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}\big((\lambda^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m})^{-1}\oplus(\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[2]\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[m])^{\oplus 2e}\big)\not=0.

(2)
Let
\ell^{\mathbb{C}}=d+1
,
r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}=0
, and
r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}=\dots=r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}=2e
.
Hence

(r_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{m-2}+\dots+r_{m-1}^{\mathbb{C}}2^{0})+r_{m}^{\mathbb{C}}-(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1+\ell^{\mathbb{C}})(2^{m-1}-1)-\ell^{\mathbb{C}}=\\
2e(2^{m-2}+\dots+2^{0})+2e-2d^{\mathbb{C}}(2^{m-1}-1)-d^{\mathbb{C}}-1=\\
2^{m}(e-d^{\mathbb{C}})+d^{\mathbb{C}}-1=d^{\mathbb{C}}-1-2^{m+t}.

Thus,
{d^{\mathbb{C}}-1-2^{m+t}\choose d^{\mathbb{C}}-1}=_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}1\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
, because
d^{\mathbb{C}}-1<2^{t+1}\leq 2^{t+m}
.
Consequently, the assumption of Lemma
6.20
is satisfied for the chosen parameters, and so

w_{2(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)}((\lambda^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m})^{-(d^{\mathbb{C}}+1)}\oplus(\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[1]\oplus\cdots\oplus\mu^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m}[m])^{\oplus 2e})\not=0.

∎

Just as in the proof of Corollary
6.14
we can derive the following estimate for non-vanishing of the relevant dual Stiefel–Whitney classes.

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
d^{\mathbb{C}}\geq 2
and
m\geq 1
be integers, and let
d^{\mathbb{C}}=2^{t}+e
for some integer
t\geq 1
and
0\leq e\leq 2^{t}-1
.
Set
d=2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1
.
Then there exist integers

a
and
b
with the property that

(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)-2(2^{m-1}-1)e\leq a\leq(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1),

(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1)-2(2^{m}-1)e\leq b\leq(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1)(2^{m}-1),

and in addition, the dual Stiefel--Whitney classes

\overline{w}_{2a}(\lambda^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m})\not=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{w}_{2b}((\lambda^{\mathbb{C}}_{d,m})^{d^{\mathbb{C}}+1})\not=0,

do not vanish.

Now, using the criteria for the existence of complex
k
-regular embeddings and complex
\ell
-skew embeddings given in Lemma
5.25
, we can directly derive the following estimates — a complex analogue of Theorem
6.16
.

Theorem 6.23
.

Let
d^{\mathbb{C}}\geq 2
,
m\geq 1
,
k\geq 1
and
\ell\geq 1
be integers, and let
d^{\mathbb{C}}=2^{t}+e
for some integer
t\geq 1
and
0\leq e\leq 2^{t}-1
.
Set
d=2d^{\mathbb{C}}-1
.
Then

(i)

there is no complex
k
-regular embedding

\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
if

N\leq(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1-e)(k-\alpha(k))+e(\alpha_{2}(k)-\epsilon(k))+k-1,

(ii)

there is no complex
\ell
-skew embedding

\mathbb{C}^{d^{\mathbb{C}}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}

if

N\leq(d^{\mathbb{C}}-1-2e)(\ell-\alpha(\ell))+(d^{\mathbb{C}}+1)\ell-2.

In a similar way as in Corollary
6.17
, Corollary
6.18
and Corollary
6.19
it can be verified that Theorem
6.23
implies Theorem
5.26
and Theorem
5.28
.

7.
Appendix

7.1.
Operads

In this section we recall basic notions from the theory of operads that we use.
We follow the framework developed by May in
[
76
]
, with some slight modifications in the notation.

Let
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op}
be the category of compactly generated weak Hausdorff spaces with continuous maps as morphisms, and let
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
denote the category of compactly generated weak Hausdorff spaces with non-degenerate base points where the morphisms are base point preserving continuous maps.
Furthermore, assume that all the products as well as function spaces are endowed with compactly generated topology.

7.1.1.
Definition and basic example

The notion of an operad and its first formal definition appeared in work of May in 1970’s.
For more details consult the original publication
[
76
, Sec. 1]
.

Definition 7.1
.

An
operad

\mathcal{O}
is given by a family of topological spaces in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op}

\mathcal{O}:=\{\mathcal{O}(n):n\geq 0\}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\mathcal{O}(0):=\{\mathrm{pt}\}

together with a family of continuous maps

\mu\colon(\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{O}(k)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(n)

where
k,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\geq 0
and
n=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
, such that the following axioms are satisfied:

(1)

For every
a\in\mathcal{O}(k)
,
b_{1}\in\mathcal{O}(n_{1}),\ldots,b_{k}\in\mathcal{O}(n_{k})
,
c_{1}\in\mathcal{O}(m_{1}),\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathcal{O}(m_{n})

\mu(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n};\mu(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k};a))=\mu(d_{1},\ldots,d_{k};a)

where for
1\leq i\leq k

d_{i}:=\begin{cases}\mu(c_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i-1}+1},\ldots,c_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i}};b_{i}),&n_{i}\neq 0\\
\mathrm{pt},&n_{i}=0.\end{cases}

Here
n=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
.

(2)

There exists an element
\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{O}(1)
with the property that for every
a\in\mathcal{O}(n)
and every
b\in\mathcal{O}(k)

\mu(a;\mathbf{1})=a,\qquad\qquad\qquad\mu(\mathbf{1},\ldots,\mathbf{1};b)=b.

(3)

Every space
\mathcal{O}(n)
is endowed with a right action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
that fulfills

\displaystyle\mu(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k};a\cdot\pi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(b_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\ldots,b_{\pi^{-1}(k)};a)\cdot\pi_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}},

\displaystyle\mu(b_{1}\cdot\pi_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\cdot\pi_{k};a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(b_{1},\ldots,b_{k};a)\cdot(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}),

where the permutation

—

\pi_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is given by permuting
k
blocks

(1,\ldots,n_{1})(n_{1}+1,\ldots,n_{1}+n_{2})\cdots(n_{1}+\cdots n_{k-1}+1,\ldots,n_{1}+\cdots n_{k})

as the permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
permutes
(1,\ldots,k)
, and

—

(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
denotes the image of
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{S}_{n_{k}}
via the inclusion
\mathfrak{S}_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{S}_{n_{k}}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

If in addition each action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on
\mathcal{O}(n)
is free the operad
\mathcal{O}
is called a
\mathfrak{S}
-free operad
.
The map
\mu_{\mathcal{O}}:=\mu
is called the
structural map
of the operad
\mathcal{O}
.

The defining requirement on the action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on
\mathcal{O}(n)
for
n\geq 0
, Definition
7.1
(
3
), directly implies the following property of the structural map.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer, and let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}
be integers such that

n_{1}=\cdots=n_{i_{1}}<n_{i_{1}+1}=\cdots=n_{i_{2}}<\cdots<n_{i_{r-1}+1}=\cdots=n_{i_{r}}

where
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{r-1}<i_{r}=k
.
Then

(1)

The product
(\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{O}(k)
is endowed with a

\mathcal{S}_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k};k}=\\
(\mathfrak{S}_{n_{i_{1}}}^{i_{1}}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{i_{1}})\times(\mathfrak{S}_{n_{i_{2}}}^{i_{2}-i_{1}+1}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{i_{2}-i_{1}+1})\times\cdots\times(\mathfrak{S}_{n_{i_{r}}}^{i_{r}-i_{r-1}+1}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{i_{r}-i_{r-1}+1})

right action.

(2)

The structural map

\mu\colon(\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{O}(k)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(n),

with
n:=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
, is an equivariant map with respect to the natural inclusion map of the groups
\mathcal{S}_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k};k}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

The first and the central example of an operad is the endomorphism operad associated with any topological space in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
.
The importance of the endomorphism operad will be become apparent after the definition of an action of an operad on a topological space.

Example 7.3
.

Let
X
be a topological space in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
.
The
endomorphism operad

\mathcal{E}nd_{X}
associated to
X
is defined as follows:

—

\mathcal{E}nd_{X}(n):=\operatorname{\mathrm{M}or}_{\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}}(X^{n},X)
where
n\geq 0
and
X^{0}:=\mathrm{pt}
,

—

\mu(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k};f):=f\circ(g_{1}\times\cdots\times g_{k})
for
f\in\mathcal{E}nd_{X}(k)
,
g_{1}\in\mathcal{E}nd_{X}(n_{1}),\ldots,g_{k}\in\mathcal{E}nd_{X}(n_{k})
, and

—

(f\cdot\pi)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}):=f(x_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi^{-1}(k)})
where
f\in\mathcal{E}nd_{X}(k)
,
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, and
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in X^{k}
.

The endomorphism operad is not a
\mathfrak{S}
-free operad.

In order to have a category of operads we introduce a notion of a morphism between operads.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{D}
be operads.
A
morphism of operads

\Phi\colon\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a family of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-equivariant maps
\Phi_{n}\colon\mathcal{O}(n)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}(n)
such that the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{O}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathcal{O}}}
\scriptstyle{(\Phi_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Phi_{n_{k}})\times\Phi_{k}}
\textstyle{\ \mathcal{O}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{n}}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{D}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{D}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{D}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\mathcal{D}}}
\textstyle{\ \mathcal{D}(n)}

for every collection of integers
k,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\geq 0
where
n:=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
.

The
category of operads

\operatorname{\mathrm{O}p}
consists of operads as objects and morphisms of operads as morphisms.

7.1.2.
\mathcal{O}
-space

An action of an operad on a topological with a base point is defined as follows.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
X
be a space in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
and let
\mathcal{O}
be an operad.
An
action of the operad

\mathcal{O}
on the space
X
is a morphism of operads
\Theta\colon\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}nd_{X}
.
The pairs
(X,\Theta)
is called an
\mathcal{O}
-space
.

A
morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-spaces

(X,\Theta)
and
(X^{\prime},\Theta^{\prime})
is a continuous based map
f\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
such that for every
n\geq 0
and every
a\in\mathcal{O}(n)
the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{X^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{n}(a)}
\scriptstyle{f^{n}}
\textstyle{\ X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{n}^{\prime}(a)}
\textstyle{\ Y.}

An equivalent definition of the action of an operad on a topological space is formulated in the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
X
be a space in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
, and let
\mathcal{O}
be an operad.
An action
\Theta\colon\mathcal{O}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}nd_{X}
of the operad
\mathcal{O}
on
X
determines and is determined by the family of continuous maps

\Theta_{n}\colon X^{n}\times\mathcal{O}(n)\longrightarrow X,

where
\Theta_{0}\colon\mathrm{pt}\longrightarrow X
is the inclusion of the base point, that satisfy the following properties:

(1)

For every
k,n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\geq 0
and
n:=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{X^{n}\times(\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{O}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\times\mu}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\ X^{n}\times\mathcal{O}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{n}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\big(X^{n_{1}}\times\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\big)\times\cdots\times\big(X^{n_{k}}\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k})\big)\times\mathcal{O}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Theta_{n_{k}}\times\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\ X^{k}\times\mathcal{O}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{k}}

where

\qquad s\colon X^{n}\times(\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{O}(k)\longrightarrow\\
\big(X^{n_{1}}\times\mathcal{O}(n_{1})\big)\times\cdots\times\big(X^{n_{k}}\times\mathcal{O}(n_{k})\big)\times\mathcal{O}(k)

is the obvious shuffle homeomorphism.

(2)

For every
x\in X

\Theta_{1}(x;\mathbf{1})=x.

(3)

For every
a\in\mathcal{O}(n)
,
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and every
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in X^{n}

\Theta_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};a\cdot\pi)=\Theta_{n}(x_{\pi^{-1}(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi^{-1}(n)};a).

Furthermore, if
f\colon(X,\Theta)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},\Theta^{\prime})
is a morphism of
\mathcal{O}
-spaces, then for every
n\geq 0
the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{X^{n}\times\mathcal{O}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{f^{n}\times\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\ X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{(X^{\prime})^{n}\times\mathcal{O}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\Theta^{\prime}_{n}}
\textstyle{\ X^{\prime}.}

7.1.3.
Little cubes operad

In this section we give an example of an operad whose structural map is studied in the central part of this work.

Let
I:=[0,1]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be the unit interval, and then
I^{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the associated
d
-cube.
Denote by
p:=(\tfrac{1}{2},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{2})\in I^{d}
the centre of the
d
-cube.
A little
d
-cube is simply an embedding of a cube into
I^{d}
in such a way that then corresponding edges are parallel, see illustration in Figure
11
.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
A
little
d
-cube
is an orientation preserving affine embedding
\vec{c}\colon I^{d}\longrightarrow I^{d}
of a
d
-cube that can be presented as the product map
\vec{c}=c_{1}\times\cdots\times c_{d}
where each
c_{i}\colon I\longrightarrow I
,
1\leq i\leq d
, is an orientation preserving affine embedding.

Figure 11.
Illustration of a
2
-cube and an element of the little
2
-cube operad.

Now, the little cubes operad is defined as follows.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
The
little
d
-cubes operad

\mathcal{C}_{d}
, for
n\geq 1
, is defined by the family of topological spaces of all
n
-tuples of little
d
-cubes whose interiors are pairwise disjoint, that is

\mathcal{C}_{d}(n):=\{\alpha:=(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n}):\vec{c}_{i}(\operatorname{int}I^{d})\cap\vec{c}_{j}(\operatorname{int}I^{d})=\varnothing\text{ for }i\neq j\}.

The space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(0)
is the point, containing the unique “embedding” of the empty set into the cubde
I^{d}
.
The
n
-tuple
\alpha=(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})
of little
d
-cubes can be seen also as a continuous map
\alpha\colon\coprod_{j=1}^{n}I^{d}\longrightarrow I^{d}
, where “
\coprod
” denotes the disjoint union.
The space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(0)
is assumed to be a point interpreted as the unique embedding of the empty set into
I^{d}
.
The space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)
is equipped with the subspace topology induced from the space of all continuous maps
\coprod_{j=1}^{n}I^{d}\longrightarrow I^{d}
.

The remaining necessary ingredients for the definition the little
d
-cube operad are given as follows.

(1)

The structural map of the little
d
-cube operad

\mu\colon(\mathcal{C}_{d}(n_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{d}(n_{k}))\times\mathcal{C}_{d}(k)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{d}(n),

n=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
, is defined as a composition

\alpha\circ(\beta_{1}\uplus\cdots\uplus\beta_{k})\colon\Big(\coprod_{j=1}^{n_{1}}I^{d}\Big)\amalg\cdots\amalg\Big(\coprod_{j=1}^{n_{k}}I^{d}\Big)\longrightarrow\Big(\coprod_{j=1}^{k}I^{d}\Big)\longrightarrow I^{d}

where
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}(k)
and
\beta_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{d}(n_{1}),\ldots,\beta_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{d}(n_{k})
.

(2)

The element
\mathbf{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{d}(1)
is the identity map
\operatorname{id}\colon I^{d}\longrightarrow I^{d}
.

(3)

The right action of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on the space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)
is given by

(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})\cdot\pi:=(\vec{c}_{\pi(1)},\ldots,\vec{c}_{\pi(n)}).

The little
d
-cubes operad is an
\mathfrak{S}
-free operad since for each
n
the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts freely on
\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)
.

There exists an
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-equivariant map of little
d
-cube operad space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)
into the configuration space
\operatorname{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d},n)
given by evaluating each cube at the centre
p=(\tfrac{1}{2},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{2})\in I^{d}
:

\operatorname{ev}_{d,n}\colon\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d},n),\qquad(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})\longmapsto(\vec{c}_{1}(p),\ldots,\vec{c}_{n}(p)).

(100)

This map is an
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence, see
[
76
, Thm. 4.8]
.

Lemma 7.9
.

For integers
d\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
the evaluation at centers of cubes map
\operatorname{ev}_{d,n}\colon\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)\longrightarrow\operatorname{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d},n)
is an
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-equivariant homotopy equivalence of the spaces
\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)
and
\operatorname{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d},n)
.

7.1.4.
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-spaces, an example

In this section we give an example of a family of
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-spaces.
More precisely, for a pointed topological space
X
we define an action of the little cubes operad
\mathcal{C}_{d}
on its
d
-fold loop space
\Omega^{d}X
.
For more details consult for example
[
76
, Thm. 5.1]
.

In order to define a
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-action on the
d
-fold loop space
\Omega^{d}X
we use Lemma
7.6
and give a family of functions

\Theta_{d,n}\colon(\Omega^{d}X)^{n}\times\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)\longrightarrow\Omega^{d}X.

For
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}\in\Omega^{d}X
and
(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})\in\mathcal{C}_{d}(n)
the loop

\Theta_{d,n}(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n};\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})\colon I^{d}\longrightarrow X

is defined, when
x\in I^{d}
, by

\Theta_{d,n}(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n};\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})(x):=\begin{cases}\omega_{i}(\vec{c}_{i}^{-1}(x)),&\text{if }x\in\vec{c}_{i}(I^{d})\text{ for some }0\leq i\leq n,\\
\mathrm{pt},&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{pt}\in X
is the base point.
In the case
d=1
the elements
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}
are pointed loops and the the map
\Theta_{1,n}(\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n};\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})
is just a concatenation of loops
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}
specified by the collection of, pairwise interior disjoint, intervals
(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{n})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(n)
.
For an illustration see Figure
12
.
By direct inspection it can be verified that just defined family of functions satisfies all the necessary conditions of Lemma
7.6
.
Thus, for a space
X
in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
the
d
-fold loop space
\Omega^{d}X
is a
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space.

Figure 12.
An illustration of the loop
\Theta_{1,3}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{1};\vec{c}_{1},\vec{c}_{2},\vec{c}_{3})
.

7.1.5.
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-spaces, a free
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space over
X

Let
X
be a pointed space with the base point
\mathrm{pt}\in X
, and let
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Then we define the
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space associated to
X
to be the quotient space

\mathcal{C}_{d}(X):=\Big(\coprod_{m\geq 0}\mathcal{C}_{d}(m)\times_{\mathfrak{S}_{m}}X^{m}\Big)/_{\approx}

where for
(\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{m})\in\mathcal{C}_{d}(m)
and
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1},\mathrm{pt})\in X^{m}
we define equivalence relation generated by

((\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{m-1},\vec{c}_{m}),(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1},\mathrm{pt}))\approx((\vec{c}_{1},\ldots,\vec{c}_{m-1}),(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1})).

The
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-action on
\mathcal{C}_{d}(X)
is induced from the structural maps of the little cubes operad
\mathcal{C}_{d}
.
For more details on the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{d}(X)
, as a monad associated to the operad
\mathcal{C}_{d}
, see
[
76
, Constr. 2.4]
.

The
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(X)
, associated to the pointed space
X
, can be called the
free
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space generated by
X
, because for every
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space
Y
there exists a bijective correspondence

(\text{morphisms of }\mathcal{C}_{d}\text{-space from }\mathcal{C}_{d}(X)\text{ to }Y)\longleftrightarrow\\
(\text{morphisms of topological space from }X\text{ to }Y).

(101)

Consult
[
76
, Prop 2.8 and Lem. 2.9]
.

Let
X\longrightarrow\Omega^{d}\Sigma^{d}X
be the map associated to the identity map
\operatorname{id}\colon\Sigma^{d}X\longrightarrow\Sigma^{d}X
along the adjunction relation

[A,\Omega^{d}B]_{\mathrm{pt}}\longleftrightarrow[\Sigma^{d}A,B]_{\mathrm{pt}}.

Here
[A,B]_{\mathrm{pt}}
denotes the set of all homotopy classes of pointed maps
A\longrightarrow B
between the pointed spaces
A
and
B
.
Next, let
\alpha_{d}\colon\mathcal{C}_{d}(X)\longrightarrow\Omega^{d}\Sigma^{d}X
denotes the morphism of
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space associated to the map
X\longrightarrow\Omega^{d}\Sigma^{d}X
with respect to the correspondence (
101
).
Now the Approximation theorem
[
76
, Thm. 2.7 and Thm. 6.1]
of May states the following.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer, or let
d=\infty
.
If
X
is a path-connected space in
\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{pt}}
, then

\alpha_{d}\colon\mathcal{C}_{d}(X)\longrightarrow\Omega^{d}\Sigma^{d}X

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

7.1.6.
Araki–Kudo–Dyer–Lashof homology operations

Following analogy with Dyer & Lashof
[
45
, Def. 2.2]
we review basic properties of the Araki–Kudo–Dyer–Lashof homology operations as defined by Cohen
[
33
, Def. 5.6]
.
In the case
p=2
the homology operations were first introduced by Araki & Kudo
[
70
]
.

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer, or let
d=\infty
.
Let
Y
be a compactly generated weak Hausdorff space with non-degenerate base point endowed with an action of the little cubes operad
\mathcal{C}_{d}
.
Then there exists a sequence of maps

Q_{i}\colon H_{j}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow H_{i+2j}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2}),\qquad\qquad 0\leq i\leq d-1,

called
Araki--Kudo--Dyer--Lashof homology operations
.
Properties of these operations are listed in the next proposition, see also
[
45
, Thm. 2.2, Cor. 1]

[
33
, Sec. 1]
.
In the following
\lambda_{d-1}\colon H_{i}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})\otimes H_{j}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow H_{i+j+d-1}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
denotes the Browder operation, consult
[
22
]

[
33
, Thm. 1.2]
.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
Y
and
Z
be
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-spaces.
The following properties hold:

(1)

Q_{0}(y)=y^{2}
for every
x\in H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
.

(2)

Q_{i}
is a homomorphism for every
0\leq i\leq d-2
.

(3)

Q_{d-1}
is not homomorphism in general and

Q_{d-1}(y_{1}+y_{2})=Q_{d-1}(y_{1})+Q_{d-1}(y_{2})+\lambda_{d-1}(y_{1},y_{2})

for
y_{1},y_{2}\in H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
with
\deg(y_{1})=\deg(y_{2})
.

(4)

If
Y
is connected and
1\in H_{0}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
, then
Q_{i}(1)=0
for
0<i\leq d-1
.

(5)

The operations
Q_{i}
are natural with respect to the morphisms of
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-spaces.

(6)

If
y_{r}\in H_{r}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
,
Z_{s}\in H_{s}(Z;\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
0\leq i\leq d-1
, then in
H_{*}(Y\times Z;\mathbb{F}_{2})
holds

Q_{i}(y_{r}\otimes z_{s})=\sum_{j=0}^{i}Q_{j}(y_{r})\otimes Q_{i-j}(z_{s})+\varepsilon_{n}(y_{r},z_{s}),

where
\varepsilon_{n}(y_{r}\otimes z_{s})
is the “error term.”
For example, in the case when
Y=Z=\Omega^{d}\Sigma^{d}S^{L}
,
d\geq 2
, and
L\geq 1
, the “error term” vanishes.

(7)

If
y_{r}\in H_{r}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
,
y_{s}^{\prime}\in H_{s}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
0\leq i\leq d-1
, then in
H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
holds

Q_{i}(y_{r}\cdot y_{s}^{\prime})=\sum_{j=0}^{i}Q_{j}(y_{r})\cdot Q_{i-j}(y_{s}^{\prime})+\varepsilon_{n}^{\prime}(y_{r},y_{s}^{\prime}),

where “
\cdot
” denotes the Pontryagin product and
\varepsilon_{n}^{\prime}(y_{r},y_{s}^{\prime})
stands for the error term.
For example, in the case when
Y=\Omega^{d}\Sigma^{d}S^{L}
,
d\geq 2
, and
L\geq 1
, the error term vanishes.

Assume that
Y
is still a
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-space.
Then the operations

Q_{i}\colon H_{j}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow H_{i+2j}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})

are defined in
[
33
, Def. 5.6]
as follows.
The
\mathcal{C}_{d}
-action on
Y
yields a map

\Theta_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}_{d}(2)\times(Y\times Y)\longrightarrow Y

that behaves naturally with respect to the acton of
\mathfrak{S}_{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Consequently, it induces the quotient map (denoted in the same way)

\Theta_{2}\colon\mathcal{C}_{d}(2)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y\times Y)\longrightarrow Y

where the action on the product
Y\times Y
is given by interchanging factors.
The space
\mathcal{C}_{d}(2)
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
equivariantly homotopic to the sphere
S^{d-1}
equipped with the antipodal actions.
Hence, the cohomology and homology of
\mathcal{C}_{d}(2)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(Y\times Y)
, with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients, is completely described in Section
3.3
.
Using the notation from Theorem
3.7
we define

Q_{i}(y):=(\Theta_{2})_{*}\big((y\otimes y)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}f_{i}\big)

for
y\in H_{*}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
where
0\leq i\leq d-1
.

7.2.
The Dickson algebra

In this section we present all the facts about the Dickson algebra that are relevant for the calculations in this paper.
For this we rely on the sources
[
2
, Sec. III.2]
,
[
27
]
, and
[
101
]
, where some of the results
we present appeared already in the original paper of Dickson
[
43
]
.

7.2.1.
Rings of invariants

Let
m\geq 1
be an integer, let
V
be an
m
-dimensional vector space over the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, and let
\mathrm{Sym}(V)
denotes the symmetric algebra of
V
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
Then for a choice of a basis
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
of
V
there is an isomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}(V)\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
.
The algebra
\mathrm{Sym}(V)
is graded by setting
\deg(x_{1})=\cdots=\deg(x_{m})=1
.
The degree of a monomial is defined in the usual way by
\deg(x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}):=\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{m}
.
The set of all homogeneous polynomials of degree
n
is denoted by
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V)
where
\mathrm{Sym}^{0}(V)\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
Consequently,
\mathrm{Sym}(V)\cong\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(V)
.

The general linear group
\operatorname{GL}(V)\cong\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
on the vector space
V
acts from the left on
\mathrm{Sym}(V)
and preserves the introduced grading.
If
G
is a subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, then
\mathrm{Sym}(V)^{G}
denotes the ring of
G
-invariants.
In this section we will discuss the invariants only of the full general linear group
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
and the subgroup
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
of all lower triangular matrices with
1
’s on the main diagonal.
It is a known fact that
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
In fact, the order of the group
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is
2^{\frac{m(m-1)}{2}}
while its index in
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is
(2m-1)!!=1\cdot 3\cdot 5\cdots(2m-1)
.

Let
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
be a fixed basis of the vector space
V
, and let us introduce a complete flag of subspaces in
V
by

\{0\}=V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{m-1}\subseteq V_{m}=V

where
V_{i}:=\operatorname{span}\{x_{m},\ldots,x_{m-i+1}\}
, for
1\leq i\leq m
.
Thus the complete flag we consider is

\{0\}\subseteq\operatorname{span}\{x_{m}\}\subseteq\operatorname{span}\{x_{m},x_{m-1}\}\subseteq\\
\cdots\subseteq\operatorname{span}\{x_{m},x_{m-1},\dots,x_{2}\}\subseteq\operatorname{span}\{x_{m},x_{m-1},\dots,x_{2},x_{1}\}.

The rings of invariants of the polynomial ring
S(V)\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
with respect to the groups
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
are denoted as follows:

\mathfrak{H}_{m}:=\mathrm{Sym}(V)^{\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]^{\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})},

and

\mathfrak{D}_{m}:=\mathrm{Sym}(V)^{\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]^{\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}.

Assuming the previously introduced notation a result of Mùi
[
82
, Thm. 6.4]
gives the following described the ring of invariants
\mathfrak{H}_{m}
, see also
[
27
, Thm. 3.1]
.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
h_{i}:=\prod_{v\in V_{i-1}}(x_{m-i+1}+v)
for
1\leq i\leq m
, a polynomial of degree
2^{i-1}
in
\mathrm{Sym}(V)\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
.
Then

\mathfrak{H}_{m}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}].

For example, if
m=3
then

h_{1}=x_{3},\qquad h_{2}=x_{2}(x_{2}+x_{3}),\qquad h_{3}=x_{1}(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{1}+x_{3})(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}).

For the ring of invariants
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
we rely on a result of Dickson
[
43
]
and have the following presentation, see also
[
101
, Thm. 1.2]
.

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
d_{m,0},\ldots,d_{m,m-1},d_{m,m}
be the polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
defined as the coefficients of the polynomial

f_{m}(T):=\prod_{v\in V}(T+v)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}d_{m,i}\,T^{2^{i}}

in
\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}][T]
.
Then
\deg(d_{m,i})=2^{m}-2^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq m
,
d_{m,m}=1
, and

\mathfrak{D}_{m}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[d_{m,0},\ldots,d_{m,m-1}].

The polynomials
d_{m,0},\ldots,d_{m,m-1}
are called the
Dickson invariants
of
\mathrm{Sym}(V)
or of the polynomial ring
\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]
.
For example, if
m=2
then

f_{2}(T)=T(T+x_{1})(T+x_{2})(T+x_{1}+x_{2})=x_{1}x_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2})T+(x_{1}x_{2}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})T^{2}.

Hence,

d_{2,0}=x_{1}x_{2}(x_{1}+x_{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad d_{2,1}=x_{1}x_{2}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}.

On the other hand
h_{1}=x_{2}
and
h_{2}=x_{1}(x_{1}+x_{2})
, and consequently

d_{2,0}=h_{1}h_{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d_{2,1}=1\cdot h_{2}+\chi_{2}h_{1}^{2},

where
\chi_{2}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{2})
is the variable substitution given by the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
.

The formula connecting generators of the rings of invariants
\mathfrak{H}_{m}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{m}
is as follows, see for more details
[
101
, Prop. 1.3 (b)]
.

Proposition 7.14
.

For
0\leq i\leq m-1
and setting
d_{m-1,-1}=0
we have that

d_{m,i}=(\chi_{m}d_{m-1,i})\,h_{m}+(\chi_{m}d_{m-1,i-1})^{2},

(102)

where
\chi_{m}\in\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is the change of variables given by the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
0&0&\cdots&1&0\\
&&\cdots&&\\
1&0&\cdots&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

If we set
k_{j}:=\chi_{m}h_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m
then the relation (
102
) becomes

d_{m,i}=d_{m-1,i}k_{m}+d_{m-1,i-1}^{2},

(103)

where
0\leq i\leq m-1
.
It is not hard to see that
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
are generators of the ring of invariants with respect to the subgroup
\mathrm{U}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
of
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
that consists of all upper triangular matrices with
1
’s on the main diagonal.
More precisely,

\mathrm{Sym}(V)^{\mathrm{U}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}]^{\mathrm{U}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})}=\mathbb{F}_{2}[k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}].

From the relation (
103
) we can derive a presentation of each Dickson invariant
d_{m,i}
in term of
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}
.
Indeed, let
0\leq r\leq m-1
be an integer, and let
J=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r})\in[m]^{r}
with
1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{r}\leq m
.
We denote particular monomials in
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}
as follows:

k[J]:=(k_{1}\cdots k_{j_{1}-1})^{2^{r}}\,(k_{j_{1}+1}\cdots k_{j_{2}-1})^{2^{r-1}}\cdots(k_{j_{r}+1}\cdots k_{m})^{2^{0}}.

Then as in
[
27
, Thm. 4.3]
we have that

\displaystyle d_{m,r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in[m]^{r}\,:\,j_{1}<\cdots<j_{r}}k[J]

(104)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in[m]^{r}\,:\,j_{1}<\cdots<j_{r}}(k_{1}\cdots k_{j_{1}-1})^{2^{r}}\,(k_{j_{1}+1}\cdots k_{j_{2}-1})^{2^{r-1}}\cdots(k_{j_{r}+1}\cdots k_{m})^{2^{0}}.

In particular,

d_{m,0}=k_{1}\cdots k_{m}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d_{m,m-1}=k_{1}^{2^{m-1}}+k_{2}^{2^{m-2}}+\cdots+k_{m}^{2^{0}}.

(105)

7.2.2.
The Dickson invariants as characteristic classes

Let
m\geq 1
be an integer.
Consider the sequence of group embeddings

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}_{m}:=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\oplus m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{reg})}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{2^{m}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(2^{m}),}

where

\bullet

(\mathrm{reg})\colon\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\oplus m}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}
is the regular embedding
that is given by the left translation action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\oplus m}
on itself, consult
[
2
, Ex. III.2.7]
, and

\bullet

\iota_{2^{m}}\colon\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{O}(2^{m})

is the embedding given by the permutation representation.

This sequence of embeddings induces a sequence of maps of the corresponding classifying spaces

\textstyle{\mathrm{B}\mathcal{E}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(\mathrm{reg})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(\iota_{2^{m}})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{O}(2^{m}).}

Let
\gamma_{2^{m}}
denotes the tautological vector bundle over
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{O}(2^{m})
, and let us denote the pullbacks as follows

\xi_{2^{m}}:=\mathrm{B}(\iota_{2^{m}})^{*}\gamma_{2^{m}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nu_{2^{m}}:=(\mathrm{B}(\iota_{2^{m}})\circ\mathrm{B}(\mathrm{reg}))^{*}\gamma_{2^{m}}.

Then the bundles
\gamma_{2^{m}}
,
\xi_{2^{m}}
and
\nu_{2^{m}}
induce the following commutative diagram of vector bundle morphisms:

\textstyle{\mathrm{E}\mathcal{E}_{m}\times_{\mathcal{E}_{m}}\mathbb{R}^{2^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\nu_{2^{m}}}
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{E}\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}\times_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}\mathbb{R}^{2^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\xi_{2^{m}}}
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{E}\mathrm{O}(2^{m})\times_{\mathrm{O}(2^{m})}\mathbb{R}^{2^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{2^{m}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}\mathcal{E}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(\mathrm{reg})}
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{B}\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(\iota_{2^{m}})}
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{B}\mathrm{O}(2^{m}).}

Recall, that the cohomology ring of the elementary abelian group
\mathcal{E}_{m}
with coefficients in the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a polynomial ring on
m
generators in degree
1
, that is
H^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{m};\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]
with
\deg(y_{1})=\cdots=\deg(y_{m})=1
.

The relationship of the introduced bundles and the Dickson invariants is given by the following theorem, see
[
72
, Lem. 3.26]
.

Theorem 7.15
.

The total Stiefel--Whitney class of the vector bundle
\nu_{2^{m}}
is

w(\nu_{2^{m}})=1+d_{m,m-1}+d_{m,m-2}+\cdots+d_{m,0},

where
d_{m,m-1},\ldots,d_{m,0}
are the Dickson invariants of
F_{2}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}]\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{E}_{m};\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
This means that

w_{i}(\nu_{2^{m}})=\begin{cases}d_{m,j},&i=2^{m}-2^{j},\ 0\leq j\leq m-1,\\
1,&i=0,\\
0,&\text{\rm otherwise}.\end{cases}

7.3.
The Stiefel–Whitney classes of the wreath square of a vector bundle

In this section we introduce notions called the wreath square of a vector bundle
[
11
, Sec. 3]
and the
(d-1)
-partial wreath square of a vector bundle.
Furthermore, we collect all necessary facts we use in Section
6
.
Our presentation partially follows
[
11
, Sec. 3]
and is given in the generality necessary for our computations.

7.3.1.
The wreath square and the
(d-1)
-partial wreath square of a vector bundle

Let
X
be a CW-complex which a priori is not finite.
The product
X\times X
has a natural action of the group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
given by interchanging the copies, that is
\omega\cdot(x_{1},x_{2}):=(x_{2},x_{1})
, where
\omega
generates
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
(x_{1},x_{2})\in X\times X
.
The product spaces
(X\times X)\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
(X\times X)\times S^{d-1}
with the diagonal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-actions are free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces.
The action on the sphere
S^{d-1}
is assumed to be antipodal.
Thus, the projection maps

p_{1}\colon(X\times X)\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow X\times X\qquad\text{and}\qquad p_{1}\colon(X\times X)\times S^{d-1}\longrightarrow X\times X

given by
(x_{1},x_{1},e)\longmapsto(x_{1},x_{2})
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map.
Here the model for
\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is assumed to be the infinite sphere
S^{\infty}:=\operatorname{colim}_{d\to\infty}S^{d-1}
equipped with the antipodal action inherited from the action on
S^{d-1}
.

Now, we define the functors

S^{2}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{cw}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{cw}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^{2,d}\colon\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{cw}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{\mathrm{T}op_{cw}}

that are on the objects given by

S^{2}X:=((X\times X)\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=(X\times X)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2},

and

S^{2,d}X:=((X\times X)\times S^{d-1})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=(X\times X)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1},

where
X
is a CW-complex and
d\geq 1
integer.
For a morphism
h\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of CW-complexes, a continuous map, we set

S^{2}h:=(h\times h)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\operatorname{id}\colon(X\times X)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow(Y\times Y)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2},

and

S^{2,d}h:=(h\times h)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\operatorname{id}\colon(X\times X)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1}\longrightarrow(Y\times Y)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1},

to be the maps induced by the product maps

(h\times h)\times\operatorname{id}\colon(X\times X)\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow(Y\times Y)\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}

and

(h\times h)\times\operatorname{id}\colon(X\times X)\times S^{d-1}\longrightarrow(Y\times Y)\times S^{d-1}

by passing to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbits.

Consider now a real
n
-dimensional vector bundle
\xi:=(E(\xi)\overset{p_{\xi}}{\longrightarrow}B(\xi))
over the CW-complex
B(\xi)
whose fiber is
F(\xi)
.
Here we denote by
E(\xi)
the total space of
\xi
, and by
p_{\xi}
the corresponding projection map.
The pull-back vector bundle
p_{1}^{*}(\xi\times\xi)
of the product vector bundle
\xi\times\xi
along the
p_{1}
:

\textstyle{E(p_{1}^{*}(\xi\times\xi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ E(\xi\times\xi)\cong E(\xi)\times E(\xi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(B(\xi)\times B(\xi))\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\ B(\xi\times\xi)\cong B(\xi)\times B(\xi),}

is equipped with a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action.
Moreover, the projection map of the pull-back bundle
E(p_{1}^{*}(\xi\times\xi))\longrightarrow(B(\xi)\times B(\xi))\times\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map.
Hence, after passing to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbits we get the
2n
-dimensional vector bundle
S^{2}\xi
over
S^{2}B(\xi)
:

E(p_{1}^{*}(\xi\times\xi))/\mathbb{Z}_{2}\longrightarrow(B(\xi)\times B(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

The bundle
S^{2}\xi
is called the
wreath square
of the vector bundle
\xi
.

Next we consider the following pull-back diagram induced by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-inclusion
i\colon S^{d-1}\longrightarrow S^{\infty}
:

\textstyle{E(((\operatorname{id}\times\operatorname{id})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}i)^{*}S^{2}\xi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ E(p_{1}^{*}(\xi\times\xi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces/\mathbb{Z}_{2}=E(S^{2}\xi)}
\textstyle{(B(\xi)\times B(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{(\operatorname{id}\times\operatorname{id})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}i}
\textstyle{\ (B(\xi)\times B(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}=B(S^{2}\xi).}

The pull-back vector bundle
((\operatorname{id}\times\operatorname{id})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}i)^{*}S^{2}\xi
is called the
(d-1)
-partial wreath square
of the vector bundle
\xi
and is denoted by
S^{2,d}\xi
.
In other words,

S^{2,d}\xi:=((\operatorname{id}\times\operatorname{id})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}i)^{*}S^{2}\xi,

with the base space
S^{2,d}B(\xi)
.

The wreath square, as well as the
(d-1)
-partial wreath square, of a vector bundle is natural with respect to morphisms of vector bundles.
Indeed, a morphism between vector bundles
\xi\longrightarrow\eta
induces morphisms between associated wreath squares
S^{2}\xi\longrightarrow S^{2}\eta
and
(d-1)
-partial wreath squares
S^{2,d}\xi\longrightarrow S^{2,d}\eta
.
These morphisms satisfy all expected properties with respect to the composition of morphisms.
Furthermore, the wreath square and the
(d-1)
-partial wreath square of a vector bundle behave naturally with respect to the Whitney sum of vector bundles.
This means that for arbitrary vector bundles
\xi
and
\eta
there are isomorphisms of vector bundles

S^{2}(\xi\oplus\eta)\cong S^{2}(\xi)\oplus S^{2}(\eta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^{2,d}(\xi\oplus\eta)\cong S^{2,d}(\xi)\oplus S^{2,d}(\eta).

(106)

7.3.2.
Cohomology of
B(S^{2}\xi)=S^{2}B(\xi)

In this section, based on the material presented in Section
3.3
, we describe the cohomology of a typical base space of the wreath square of a vector bundle.

Let
X
be the base space of the vector bundle
\xi
, that is
X=B(\xi)
.
Assume that
X
in addition is a CW-complex.
Then the base space of the vector bundle
S^{2}\xi
is
S^{2}X
, and also the total space of the fiber bundle

\textstyle{X\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ (X\times X)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{B}\mathbb{Z}_{2}.}

(107)

Note that similarly the base space of the vector bundle
S^{2,d}\xi
is the total space of the fiber bundle

\textstyle{X\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ (X\times X)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ \mathbb{R}{\rm P}^{d-1}.}

(108)

The Serre spectral sequence associated to the fiber bundle (
107
) has
E_{2}
-term given by

E_{2}^{i,j}(X)=H^{i}(\mathrm{B}\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathcal{H}^{j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2}))\cong H^{i}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};H^{j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2})).

(109)

As discussed in Section
3.3
this spectral sequence collapses at the
E_{2}
-term, that means
E_{2}^{i,j}(X)\cong E_{\infty}^{i,j}(X)
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For more details consult Theorem
3.4
or
[
2
, Thm. IV.1.7]
.

In the description of the total Stiefel--Whitney classes of the wreath square of a vector bundle the following maps turn out to be very useful.
At first, consider the map (not a homomorphism)

P\colon H^{j}(X;\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow H^{2j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\cong E^{0,2j}_{2}\cong E^{0,2j}_{\infty}

given by

P(u):=u\otimes u,

for
u\in H^{j}(X;\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
j\geq 0
an integer.
By a direct inspection we see that the map
P
is not an additive map, but it is a multiplicative map.
The second map we consider is

T\colon H^{j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow H^{j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}

defined by

T(u\otimes u^{\prime}):=u\otimes u^{\prime}+u^{\prime}\otimes u,

where
u\otimes u^{\prime}\in H^{j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
j\geq 0
is an integer.
The map
T
is an additive map, but not a multiplicative map.

With the help of just introduced maps
P
and
T
, based on Lemma
3.2
and Theorem
3.4
, we can describe the
E_{\infty}
-term of the Serre spectral sequence (
68
) as follows:

—

E^{*,0}_{2}\cong E^{*,0}_{\infty}\cong H^{*}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[f]
,
\deg(f)=1
,

—

E^{0,*}_{2}\cong E^{0,*}_{\infty}\cong H^{*}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2})^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
,

—

E^{0,j}_{2}\cong E^{0,j}_{\infty}\cong P(H^{j/2}(X;\mathbb{F}_{2}))\oplus T(H^{j}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2}))
for
j\geq 2
even,

—

E^{i,j}_{2}\cong E^{i,j}_{\infty}\cong P(H^{j/2}(X;\mathbb{F}_{2}))\otimes H^{*}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{F}_{2})
for
j\geq 2
even, and
i\geq 1
.

Furthermore, we have that the generator
f
of
H^{*}(\mathbb{Z}_{2};\mathbb{F}_{2})
annihilates the image of the map
T
, that is

T(H^{*}(X\times X;\mathbb{F}_{2}))\cdot f=0.

(110)

Note that the Serre spectral sequence associated to the fiber bundle (
108
) can be describe in a similar way — as discussed in Section
3.3
.

7.3.3.
The total Stiefel–Whitney class of the wreath square of a vector bundle

Let
\xi
be a real
n
-dimensional vector bundle over the CW-complex
B(\xi)
.
To the vector bundle
\xi
we associate characteristic classes

s^{2}(\xi):=w(S^{2}\xi)\in H^{*}(B(S^{2}\xi);\mathbb{F}_{2}),

and

s^{2,d}(\xi):=w(S^{2,d}\xi)\in H^{*}(B(S^{2,d}\xi);\mathbb{F}_{2}).

These are the total Stiefel--Whitney classes of the real
2n
-dimensional vector bundles
S^{2}\xi
and
S^{2,d}\xi
.
The assignments

\xi\longmapsto s^{2}(\xi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\xi\longmapsto s^{2,d}(\xi)

are natural with respect to the continuous maps.
This means that for a continuous map
h\colon K\longrightarrow B(\xi)
from a CW-complex
K
into the base space
B(\xi)
of
\xi
the following equalities hold

(S^{2}h)^{*}(s^{2}(\xi))=s^{2}(h^{*}\xi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(S^{2,d}h)^{*}(s^{2,d}(\xi))=s^{2,d}(h^{*}\xi)

Here
h^{*}\xi
denotes the pull-back vector bundle of
\xi
along the map
h
.

The characteristic class
s^{2}(\xi)
was calculated, in term of Stiefel--Whitney classes of
\xi
, in
[
11
, Thm. 3.4]
and is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 7.16
.

Let
\xi
be a real
n
-dimensional vector bundle over a
CW
-complex.
Then

s^{2}(\xi)=w(S^{2}\xi)=\sum_{0\leq r<s\leq n}T(w_{r}(\xi)\otimes w_{s}(\xi))+\sum_{0\leq r\leq n}P(w_{r}(\xi))\cdot(1+f)^{n-r}

In particular,
\xi
is
1
dimensional, then

s^{2}(\xi)=w(S^{2}\xi)=T(w_{0}(\xi)\otimes w_{1}(\xi))+P(w_{0}(\xi))\cdot(1+f)+P(w_{1}(\xi))=\\
1+\big(f+1\otimes w_{1}(\xi)+w_{1}(\xi)\otimes 1\big)+w_{1}(\xi)\otimes w_{1}(\xi).

From the computations in Section
3.3
and the fact that by definition
S^{2,d}\xi=((\operatorname{id}\times\operatorname{id})\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}i)^{*}S^{2}\xi
we get the following consequence of Theorem
7.16
.

Corollary 7.17
.

Let
\xi
be a real
n
-dimensional vector bundle over a
CW
-complex, and let
d\geq 2
be an integer.
Then

s^{2,d}(\xi)=w(S^{2,d}\xi)=\\
\sum_{0\leq r<s\leq n}T(w_{r}(\xi)\otimes w_{s}(\xi))+\sum_{0\leq r\leq n}\,\sum_{0\leq j\leq\min\{n-r,d-1\}}{n-r\choose j}P(w_{r}(\xi))f^{j}.

In particular, Theorem
7.16
and Corollary
7.17
give formulas for the evaluation of mod
2
Euler classes (top Stiefel--Whitney class) of the vector bundles
S^{2}\xi
and
S^{2,d}\xi
in term of the mod
2
Euler class of the vector bundle
\xi
.
In the following we present these formulas and show them with a direct proof which does not rely on the previous claims.

Corollary 7.18
.

Let
\xi
be a real
n
-dimensional vector bundle over a
CW
-complex.
Then

\mathfrak{e}(S^{2}\xi)=P(\mathfrak{e}(\xi))\qquad\text{or}\qquad w_{2n}(S^{2}\xi)=P(w_{n}(\xi)),

and

\mathfrak{e}(S^{2,d}\xi)=P(\mathfrak{e}(\xi))\qquad\text{or}\qquad w_{2n}(S^{2,d}\xi)=P(w_{n}(\xi)).

In particular, if
\mathfrak{e}(\xi)\neq 0
, then
\mathfrak{e}(S^{2}\xi)\neq 0
and
\mathfrak{e}(S^{2,d}\xi)\neq 0
.

Here
\mathfrak{e}(S^{2}\xi)
,
\mathfrak{e}(S^{2,d}\xi)
and
\mathfrak{e}(\xi)
denote mod
2
Euler classes of the vector bundles
S^{2}\xi
,
S^{2,d}\xi
and
\xi
, respectively.
Note the abuse of notation: the map
P
is not the same in the formulas for
\mathfrak{e}(S^{2}\xi)
and
\mathfrak{e}(S^{2,d}\xi)
, since it operates on different spectral sequences.

Proof.

Let
u\in H^{n}(D(\xi),S(\xi);\mathbb{F}_{2})
be the Thom class of the
n
-dimensional vector bundle
\xi
.
Here
D(\xi)
and
S(\xi)
denote respectively the disk and sphere bundles associate to the vector bundle
\xi
.
The mod
2
Euler class of the vector bundle
\xi
, by definition, equals to
\mathfrak{e}(\xi)=i_{\xi}^{*}(u)
where
i_{\xi}\colon(E(\xi),\varnothing)\longrightarrow(D(\xi),S(\xi))
is the zero section.

Consider the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{n}(D(\xi),S(\xi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{i_{\xi}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{\ H^{n}(E(\xi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{H^{2n}(((D(\xi),S(\xi))\times(D(\xi),S(\xi)))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{(Pi_{\xi})^{*}}
\textstyle{\ H^{2n}((E(\xi)\times E(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2n}(((D(\xi),S(\xi))\times(D(\xi),S(\xi)))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{(Pi_{\xi})^{*}}
\textstyle{\ H^{2n}((E(\xi)\times E(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2n}(D(S^{2}\xi),S(\xi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{i_{S^{2}\xi}^{*}}
\textstyle{\ H^{2n}((E(\xi)\times E(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathrm{E}\mathbb{Z}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2n}(D(S^{2,d}\xi),S(\xi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{i_{S^{2,d}\xi}^{*}}
\textstyle{\ H^{2n}((E(\xi)\times E(\xi))\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}S^{d-1})}

where the coefficients are assumed to be in the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
Note that the curly left arrows are isomorphism while the right ones are equalities.

We claim that
P(u)
, appropriately interpreted, is the Thom class of the vector bundle
S^{2}\xi
, respectively
S^{2,d}\xi
.
To check this it is enough to show that it restricts to the generator in each fibre.
Thus we can reduce to the case in which
E(\xi)
and
\mathrm{B}\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, respectively
\mathbb{R}{\rm P}^{d-1}
, are just points.
Then it is an elementary fact that the square map
P
:

\big(H^{n}(D(\mathbb{R}^{n}),S(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}\big)\longrightarrow\big(H^{2n}(D(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),S(\mathbb{R}^{2n});\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}\big)

maps the generator to the generator.

The assertion now follows from the commutativity of the correspond diagram:

\mathfrak{e}(S^{2}\xi)=i_{S^{2}\xi}^{*}(P(u))=P(i_{\xi}^{*}(u))=P(\mathfrak{e}(\xi)),

and similarly

\mathfrak{e}(S^{2,d}\xi)=i_{S^{2,d}\xi}^{*}(P(u))=P(i_{\xi}^{*}(u))=P(\mathfrak{e}(\xi)).

∎

7.4.
Miscellaneous calculations

In order give a smoother presentation of the main computational components in the main body of the paper in this section we present details of some auxiliary computations.

7.4.1.
Detecting group cohomology

In this section we review some basic facts from classical work of Quillen
[
90
]
in generality we need in this paper.
For more details consult for example
[
2
, Sec. IV.4 and VI.1]
.

Let
G
be a finite group, and let
p
be a prime.
The family of subgroups
\{H_{i}:i\in I\}
of the group
G

detects the cohomology
of
G
modulo
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, or is a
detecting family of subgroups
, if the homomorphism

H^{*}(G;\mathbb{F}_{p})\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}H^{*}(H_{i};\mathbb{F}_{p})

induced by the restrictions
\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H_{i}}
is a monomorphism.
If
G^{(p)}
is Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G
, then the restriction
\operatorname{res}^{G}_{G^{(p)}}
is a monomorphism, and consequently
G^{(p)}
detects the cohomology of
G
modulo
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

First we recall an auxiliary lemma that is particularly useful for us, see
[
72
, Lem. 3.22]
.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
G
be a finite group, and let
p
be a prime.
Then
G\wr\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is detected by
G\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\underbrace{G\times\cdots\times G}_{p\text{ times}}
.

Next, let
p
be a prime, and let
n=p^{m}
for an integer
m\geq 1
.
Consider symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m}}
as a group of permutation of the set
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\oplus m}
.
Then the elementary abelian group
\mathcal{E}_{m}
of all translations (seen as permutations) of the vector space
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\oplus m}
, the so called regular embedded subgroup
[
2
, Ex. III.2.7]
, is isomorphic to the elementary abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\oplus m}
.
Let

\mathcal{S}_{p^{m}}=E_{m,1}\wr\cdots\wr E_{m,m}\cong\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}_{p}\wr\cdots\wr\mathbb{Z}_{p}}_{m\text{ times}}

denotes the Sylow
p
-subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m}}
containing
\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
Here
E_{m,i}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is the subgroup of
\mathcal{E}_{m}
generated by the
i
th basis element
(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots,0)
of the vector space
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\oplus m}
.

Now we proceed with the following detection property of the cohomology of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m}}
and its Sylow
p
-subgroup
\mathcal{S}_{p^{m}}
, consult
[
2
, Cor. VI.1.4]
.

Theorem 7.20
.

Let
p
be a prime, and let
n=p^{m}
for an integer
m\geq 1
.

(1)

The cohomology
H^{*}(\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m}};\mathbb{F}_{p})
of the symmetric group

\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m}}
is detected modulo
\mathbb{F}_{p}
by the elementary abelian subgroup
\mathcal{E}_{m}
and the product subgroup

\underbrace{\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m-1}}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m-1}}}_{p\text{ times}}.

(2)

The cohomology
H^{*}(\mathcal{S}_{p^{m}};\mathbb{F}_{p})
of the Sylow
p
-subgroup

\mathcal{S}_{p^{m}}
of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{p^{m}}
is detected modulo
\mathbb{F}_{p}
by the elementary abelian subgroup
\mathcal{E}_{m}
and the product subgroup

\underbrace{\mathcal{S}_{p^{m-1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{p^{m-1}}}_{p\text{ times}}.

Finally we state the classical result of Quillen about detection with the family of elementary abelian subgroups, see
[
2
, Thm. VI.1.2]
.

Theorem 7.21
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer, and let
p
be a prime.
The cohomology
H^{*}(\mathfrak{S}_{n};\mathbb{F}_{p})
of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
coefficients is detected by the family of its elementary abelian
p
-subgroups.

7.4.2.
The image of a restriction homomorphism

Let
G
be a finite group and let
H
a subgroup of
G
.
In this section we want to give a description of the image of the restriction homomorphism

\operatorname{im}\big(\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}\colon H^{*}(G;M)\longrightarrow H^{*}(H;M)\big)

where
M
is a trivial
G
-module.

Let
G
be a (finite) group.
Any contractible free
G
-CW complex equipped with the right
G
cellular action is called a model for an
\mathrm{E}G
space.
The Milnor model is given by
\mathrm{E}G=\operatorname{colim}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G^{*n}
where
G
stands for a
0
-dimensional free
G
-simplicial complex whose vertices are indexed by the elements of the group
G
and the action on
G
is given by the right translation, and
G^{*n}
is an
n
-fold join of the
0
-dimensional simplicial complex with induced diagonal (right) action.
A typical point in
\mathrm{E}G
can be presented as follows

\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g_{i}\equiv(\lambda_{1}g_{1},\lambda_{2}g_{2},\lambda_{3}g_{3},\ldots),

where
g_{i}\in G
and
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\geq 1
, the set
I:=\{\lambda_{i}\neq 0:i\geq 1\}
is finite, and
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{i}=1
.
The quotient space
\mathrm{B}G=\mathrm{E}G/G
is called a classifying space a the group
G
.
For a trivial
G
-module
M
the group cohomology
H^{*}(G;M)
can be defined as a singular cohomology
H^{*}(\mathrm{B}G;M)
.

Let
H
and
G
be (finite) groups, and let
f\colon H\longrightarrow G
be a homomorphism.
Then
f
induces the following
G
-equivariant map
\mathrm{E}(f)\colon\mathrm{E}H\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G
by

\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}h_{i}\equiv(\lambda_{1}h_{1},\lambda_{2}h_{2},\lambda_{3}h_{3},\ldots)\longmapsto\\
\sum_{i\geq 1}\lambda_{i}g(h_{i})\equiv(\lambda_{1}f(h_{1}),\lambda_{2}f(h_{2}),\lambda_{3}f(h_{3}),\ldots)

where the
H
-action on
G
is induced by the homomorphism
f
.
Since the map
\mathrm{E}(f)
is
H
-equivariant map it induces a map between quotient spaces
\mathrm{B}(f)\colon\mathrm{B}H\longrightarrow\mathrm{B}G
.
In particular, if
H
is a subgroup of
G
,
i\colon H\longrightarrow G
the inclusion map, then the induced homomorphism in cohomology
\mathrm{B}(i)^{*}
by definition is the restriction homomorphism
\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}
, that is
\mathrm{B}(i)^{*}=\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}
.

We prove an auxiliary lemma following
[
44
, Prop. I.6.14]
and
[
2
, Thm. II.1.9]
.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
G
be a finite group.

(i)

Any two continuous
G
-equivariant maps
f,g\colon\mathrm{E}G\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G
are
G
-homotopic.

(ii)

Let
a\in G
, and let
k_{a}\colon G\longrightarrow G
be the conjugation homomorphism
k_{a}(g):=aga^{-1}
.
Then the induced map
\mathrm{B}(k)\colon\mathrm{B}G\longrightarrow\mathrm{B}G
is homotopic to the identity
\operatorname{id}\colon\mathrm{B}G\longrightarrow\mathrm{B}G
.

Proof.

(i) This is a presentation of the proof of
[
44
, Prop. I.6.14]
.
For
e\in\mathrm{E}G
let us denote images
f(e)
and
g(e)
as follows

f(e)=(\lambda_{1}(e)f_{1}(e),\lambda_{2}(e)f_{2}(e),\ldots)\quad\text{and}\quad g(e)=(\mu_{1}(e)g_{1}(e),\mu_{2}(e)g_{2}(e),\ldots).

We define two additional maps
\bar{f},\bar{g}\colon\mathrm{E}G\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G
by

\bar{f}(e)=(\lambda_{1}(e)f_{1}(e),0,\lambda_{2}(e)f_{2}(e),0,\ldots)

and

\bar{g}(e)=(0,\mu_{1}(e)g_{1}(e),0,\mu_{2}(e)g_{2}(e),0,\ldots).

The pairs of maps
f
,
\bar{f}
and
g
,
\bar{g}
are
G
-homotopic, that is
f\simeq_{G}\bar{f}
and
g\simeq_{G}\bar{g}
.
In order to construct a
G
-homotopy, for example, between
f
and
\bar{f}
we proceed as follows.
Let
j\geq 1
be an integer and let the
G
-homotopy
H_{j}\colon\mathrm{E}G\times I\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G
be defined by

H_{j}(e,t):=\big(\lambda_{1}(e)f_{1}(e),\ldots,\lambda_{j}(e)f_{j}(e),\\
t\lambda_{j+1}(e)f_{j+1}(e),(1-t)\lambda_{j+1}(e)f_{j+1}(e),\\
t\lambda_{j+2}(e)f_{j+2}(e),(1-t)\lambda_{j+2}(e)f_{j+2}(e),\ldots\big).

Starting with
\bar{f}(e)=H_{1}(e,0)
and applying consecutively
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots
we reach
f(e)
.
Hence we have define a
G
-homotopy between
\bar{f}
and
f
.
Since in the presentation of every point
e=(\lambda_{1}g_{1},\lambda_{2}g_{2},\ldots)\in\mathrm{E}G
there are only finitely many non-zero
\lambda
’s the definition we just gave is correct and the map is moreover continuous.
Therefore,
f\simeq_{G}\bar{f}
and
g\simeq_{G}\bar{g}
.

It suffices to prove that
\bar{f}\simeq_{G}\bar{g}
.
For this we give the
G
-homotopy
H\colon\mathrm{E}G\times I\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G
by

H(e,t):=\big((1-t)\lambda_{1}(e)f_{1}(e),\,t\mu_{1}(e)g_{1}(e),\,(1-t)\lambda_{2}(e)f_{2}(e),\,t\mu_{2}(e)g_{2}(e),\ldots\big).

This concludes the proof of part (i).

(ii) The homomorphisms
\operatorname{id}\colon G\longrightarrow G
and
k_{a}\colon G\longrightarrow G
induce
G
-equivariant maps

\mathrm{E}(\operatorname{id})=\operatorname{id}\colon\mathrm{E}G\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{E}(k_{a})\colon\mathrm{E}G\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}G.

From the part (i) of this lemma we have that
\mathrm{E}(\operatorname{id})=\operatorname{id}
and
\mathrm{E}(k_{a})
are
G
-homotopic.
Consequently,
\mathrm{B}(\operatorname{id})=\operatorname{id}
and
\mathrm{B}(k_{a})
are homotopic.
∎

Now we are ready to give a description of the image a restriction that we use in the central part of the paper.
Consult
[
2
, Lem. II.3.1]
.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
H
its subgroup,
N_{G}(H)
the normalizer of
H
in
G
,
W_{G}(H):=N_{G}(H)/H
the corresponding Weyl group, and
M
a trivial
G
-module.
There is an action of
W_{G}(H)
on
H^{*}(H;M)
such that

\operatorname{im}\big(\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}\colon H^{*}(G;M)\longmapsto H^{*}(H;M)\big)\subseteq H^{*}(H;M)^{W_{G}(H)}.

Proof.

First we introduce an action of the normalizer
N_{G}(H)
on
\mathrm{B}H
.
Let
a\in N_{G}(H)
, and let
k_{a}\colon H\longrightarrow H
be a homomorphism defined by
k_{a}(h):=aha^{-1}
for
h\in H
.
For
a,b\in N_{G}(H)
the following relation obviously holds
k_{ab}=k_{a}\circ k_{b}
.
Applying the functor
\mathrm{B}
on the maps
k_{a}
, for every
a\in N_{G}(H)
, we get an action of
N_{G}(H)
on
\mathrm{B}H
.
This action naturally extends to an action on the cohomology

N_{G}(H)\times H^{*}(H;M)\longrightarrow H^{*}(H;M).

In the case when
a\in H\subseteq N_{G}(H)
from Lemma
7.22
we get that the homomorphism
\mathrm{B}(k_{a})^{*}\colon H^{*}(H;M)\longrightarrow H^{*}(H;M)
is the identity.
Consequently the action of
N_{G}(H)
factors through an action of the Weyl group
W_{G}(H)
, that gives us a commutative diagram

\textstyle{N_{G}(H)\times H^{*}(H;M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ H^{*}(H;M)}
\textstyle{W_{G}(H)\times H^{*}(H;M).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The group
N_{G}(H)
acts on
\mathrm{B}G
in the identical way.
Now for
a\in N_{G}(H)
we set
k_{a}\colon G\longrightarrow G
to be again defined by
k_{a}(g):=aga^{-1}
for
g\in G
.
Applying the functor
\mathrm{B}
we now get an action of
N_{G}(H)
on
\mathrm{B}G
and consequently on
H^{*}(G;M)
.
In this case Lemma
7.22
implies that each map
\mathrm{B}(k_{a})
is homotopic to the identity.
Hence, the induced action of
N_{G}(H)
on
H^{*}(G;M)
is a trivial action.

Since the action of
N_{G}(H)
on
\mathrm{B}G
is an extension of the action of
N_{G}(H)
on
\mathrm{B}H
we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{B}H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(k_{a})}
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{B}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(k_{a})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{B}H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{B}(i)}
\textstyle{\ \mathrm{B}G}

(111)

for every
a\in N_{G}(H)
where
i\colon H\longrightarrow G
is the inclusion.
Applying the cohomology functor on the commutative diagram (
111
) we get that

\mathrm{B}(i)^{*}\circ\mathrm{B}(k_{a})^{*}=\mathrm{B}(k_{a})^{*}\circ\mathrm{B}(i)^{*}.

Since
\mathrm{B}(i)^{*}=\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}
, and
\mathrm{B}(k_{a})^{*}\colon H^{*}(G;M)\longrightarrow H^{*}(G;M)
is the identity for every
a\in N_{G}(H)
, we get that
\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}=\mathrm{B}(k_{a})^{*}\circ\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}
, and consequently

\operatorname{im}\big(\operatorname{res}^{G}_{H}\colon H^{*}(G;M)\longmapsto H^{*}(H;M)\big)\subseteq H^{*}(H;M)^{N_{G}(H)}=H^{*}(H;M)^{W_{G}(H)}.

∎

7.4.3.
Weyl groups of an elementary abelian group

In this section we identify Weyl groups
W_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
and
W_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
for every
m\geq 1
.
First, following Mùi
[
82
, Proof of Lem. II.5.1]
we prove the following fact.

Lemma 7.24
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer.
Then

W_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\cong\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

Proof.

Let
\kappa\colon N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
be the homomorphism defined by
\kappa(a):=k_{a}
, where as before
k_{a}\colon\mathcal{E}_{m}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{m}
is the conjugation automorphism
k_{a}(e)=aea^{-1}
,
e\in\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
The kernel of the homomorphism
\kappa
is the centralizer of
\mathcal{E}_{m}
in
\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}
, which is
\ker(\kappa)=C_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
.
Furthermore, in our situation,
C_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})=\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
Thus, there is an exact sequence of groups

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ \mathcal{E}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\mathbb{N}_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ \operatorname{im}(\kappa)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ 1.}

(112)

In other words
N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
is a semi-direct product
\mathcal{E}_{m}\rtimes\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
.
Consequently,

W_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})=N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})/\mathcal{E}_{m}\cong\operatorname{im}(\kappa).

Since
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
we can say that
\operatorname{im}(\kappa)\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
and therefore the Weyl group
W_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
can be seen as a subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
Thus it remains to identify
\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
, and this will be done in two steps.

First we prove that
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
using the induction on
m\geq 1
.
Notice that obviously
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
.
For
m=1
the group
\mathrm{L}_{1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is trivial group while
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{S}_{2}=N_{\mathcal{S}_{2}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Let us assume that for
m\geq 2
the following inclusions
\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m-1})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}}
holds.
Consider the embedding
\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\longrightarrow\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
of group given by

A\longmapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&A\\
\end{array}\right)

for
A\in\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
.
Note the “difference” between zeroes in the upper matrix.
Since for every
i\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m-1}

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&A\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i\\
e\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i\\
Ae\end{array}\right)

we have that
\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is a subgroup of
\delta(\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}}\times\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}}
where
\delta
denotes the diagonal embedding.
An arbitrary element of the group
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
can be presented in the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
a&A\\
\end{array}\right)

where
A\in\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m-1}
.
Then for
i\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m-1}
we have

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
a&A\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i\\
e\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i\\
Ae+ai\end{array}\right).

Consequently,
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq(\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cdot\mathcal{E}_{m-1})\times(\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cdot\mathcal{E}_{m-1})
.
From the induction hypothesis we have more

\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq(\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cdot\mathcal{E}_{m-1})\times(\mathrm{L}_{m-1}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cdot\mathcal{E}_{m-1})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}}\times\mathcal{S}_{2^{m-1}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}.

As we have seen
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
and thus

\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m}),

which concludes the induction.

Now we continue identification of
\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
.
Since
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}=\{1\}
we have that the exact sequence (
112
) gives us an embedding of
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
into
\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
.
Hence we have the following inclusions of
2
-groups (with the obvious abuse of notation)

\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\kappa)\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

The image
\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
is a
2
-group as an image of the
2
-group
N_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
.
Because
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
is a Sylow
2
-subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
we have that
\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})=\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
and consequently

W_{\mathcal{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\cong\operatorname{im}(\kappa)\cong\mathrm{L}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

∎

Next, adapting the proof of the previous lemma and following
[
2
, Ex. III.2.7]
we determine the Weyl group of
\mathcal{E}_{m}
now inside the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}
.

Lemma 7.25
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer.
Then

W_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2}).

Proof.

Let
\kappa\colon N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
be the homomorphism defined by
\kappa(a):=k_{a}
, where as before
k_{a}\colon\mathcal{E}_{m}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{m}
is the conjugation automorphism
k_{a}(e)=aea^{-1}
,
e\in\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
As in the proof of the previous lemma
\ker(\kappa)=C_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})=\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
Hence, we get an exact sequence of groups

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ \mathcal{E}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ \operatorname{im}(\kappa)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

Consequently,
N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
is a semi-direct product
\mathcal{E}_{m}\rtimes\operatorname{im}(\kappa)
implying that

W_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})=N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})/\mathcal{E}_{m}\cong\operatorname{im}(\kappa).

Since
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{m})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
, in order to complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to prove that
\operatorname{im}(\kappa)=\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
.
For that fix
\alpha\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
, and denote by
\bar{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}
the permutation given with
e\longmapsto\alpha(e)
for
e\in\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
Here we use the fact that
\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}
is group of permutations of the set
\mathcal{E}_{m}
.
Then
\bar{\alpha}\in N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
and
\kappa(\bar{\alpha})=\alpha
.
Indeed, for
e,h\in\mathcal{E}_{m}
holds:

\bar{\alpha}e\bar{\alpha}^{-1}(h)=(\bar{\alpha}e)(\alpha^{-1}(h))=\bar{\alpha}(e+\alpha^{-1}(h))=\alpha(e+\alpha^{-1}(h))=\alpha(e)+h.

consequently we have an equality of permutations
\bar{\alpha}e\bar{\alpha}^{-1}=\alpha(e)\in\mathcal{E}_{m}
that implies
\bar{\alpha}\in N_{\mathfrak{S}_{2^{m}}}(\mathcal{E}_{m})
and
\kappa(\bar{\alpha})=\alpha
.
This concludes the proof of surjectivity of
\kappa
and of the lemma.
∎

7.4.4.
Cohomology of the real projective space with local coefficients

For
d\geq 2
we compute the cohomology of the projective space

H^{*}(\mathbb{R}{\rm P}^{d-1};\mathcal{M})

where the local coefficient system
\mathcal{M}
is additively
\mathbb{F}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}
, and the action of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{R}{\rm P}^{d-1})=\langle t\rangle
on
\mathcal{M}
is given by
t\cdot(a_{1},a_{2})=(a_{2},a_{1})
where
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

We consider two separate cases:
d=2
when
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{R}{\rm P}^{d-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and
d\geq 3
when
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{R}{\rm P}^{d-1})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
First we recall the definition of the cohomology with local coefficients, consult for example
[
60
, Sec. 3.H]
.

Let
X
be a path-connected CW-complex,
\widetilde{X}
its universal cover, and let
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
be its fundamental group.
Denote by
\mathcal{L}
a local coefficient system on
X
, that is a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module.
Assume that we are given a structure of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-CW-complex on
\widetilde{X}
with associated cellular chain complex

\textstyle{\cdots\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ C_{n+1}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{d_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\ C_{n}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{d_{n}}
\textstyle{\ C_{n-1}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{d_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\ \cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{d_{2}}
\textstyle{\ C_{1}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
\textstyle{\ C_{0}(\widetilde{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ }
\textstyle{\ 0\ ,}

where
C_{n}(\widetilde{X})
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module, and
d_{n}\colon C_{n}(\widetilde{X})\longrightarrow C_{n-1}(\widetilde{X})
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module homomorphism, for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The cohomology
H^{*}(X;\mathcal{L})
of
X
with local coefficients in
\mathcal{L}
is cohomology of the cochain complex

\textstyle{\cdots\ }
\textstyle{\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hom_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(C_{n+1}(\widetilde{X});\mathcal{L})\ }
\textstyle{\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hom_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(C_{n}(\widetilde{X});\mathcal{L})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hom_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(C_{n-1}(\widetilde{X});\mathcal{L})}
\textstyle{\cdots\ }
\textstyle{\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hom_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi]}(C_{1}(\widetilde{X});\mathcal{L})\ }
